## Mental model

Задача сводится к **дезопперации (disruption) side-эффектов от пользовательского запроса**: ты хочешь, чтобы `register()` гарантированно сохранил пользователя, но отправка письма и индексация были *отложенными*, фоновыми и отказоустойчивыми. Лучший инструмент в Symfony для этого — **Messenger**, а не EventDispatcher (который сам по себе синхронный).

---

## Вариант A: Messenger + async transport (recommended)

```php
// Domain — доменно-нейтральное событие
final class UserWasRegistered {
    public function __construct(public readonly string $userId) {}
}

// RegisterController / UserRegistry
final class RegisterController {
    public function __construct(private UserBus $bus) {}

    public function __invoke(Request $request): JsonResponse {
        $user = $this->registry->register($request->toArray());

        // Dispatch — и письмо, и индексация уходят в queue, ответ мгновенный
        $this->bus->dispatch(new UserWasRegistered($user->id));

        return JsonResponse::fromJsonString($user->toArray(), 201);
    }
}
```

```yaml
# messenger.yaml
framework:
    messenger:
        transports:
            async: '://%ENV(MESSENGER_TRANSPORT_DSN)%'  # amqp://, doctrine://://main, redis://
            # или для dev: async: 'doctrine://main'
        routing:
            'App\Domain\Event\UserWasRegistered': async
```

```php
// Handler
final class SendWelcomeEmailAndIndexHandler {
    public function __invoke(UserWasRegistered $message): void {
        $this->mailer->sendWelcome($message->userId);
        $this->indexer->refresh($message->userId); // async: не блокирует ответ
    }
}
```

**Запуск consumer'а (отдельный процесс):**
```bash
php bin/console messenger:consume async --memory-limit=128M --time-limit=...
```
Запускается через supervisor/systemd, может быть сколько угодно worker'ов (horizontal scaling).

### Trade-offs Messenger
- **+** надёжность (at-least-once), retry через `failed` transport, delayed delivery, multiple consumers, observable (broker).
- **–** extra infra (брокер), eventual consistency (индекс обновится не сразу), сложнее дебажить, нужна идемпотентность consumer'а.

---

## Вариант B: Doctrine transport (без брокера)

```yaml
messenger:
    transports:
        async: 'doctrine://main?queue_name=async'
```

- Очереди живут в таблице БД (`messenger_messages`).
- **Но:** worker всё равно нужен — `messenger:consume` **не async сам по себе**.
- Плюсы: ноль доп. инфраструктуры, легко в dev/small app.
- Минусы: очередь в твоей БД, при росте нагрузка на БД, сложнее масштабировать.

> Честная оговорка: doctrine transport — это «очередь в БД», не замена AMQP в production под нагрузкой.

---

## Вариант C: Event Dispatcher (⚠️ НЕ async сам по себе)

```php
// Валидно, но LISTENER выполняется INLINE в том же request → тормозит ответ!
$dispatcher->dispatch(new UserWasRegistered($user->id));
// listener делает mailer + indexer → всё синхронно
```

EventDispatcher синхронный. Единственный способ сделать его async — **мост в Messenger**:

```php
// listener → dispatches message на bus
public function onUserRegistered(UserWasRegistered $e): void {
    $this->bus->dispatch($e);
}
```

Или (костыльно) — spawn фоновый процесс через `proc_open`, что я не рекомендую.

**Trade-off:** Events хороши для *внутреннего* coupling (множество реакций на событие), но для async нужен Messenger.

---

## Вариант D: Delay / batching для индекса

Индексация — **тяжёлая и не realtime**. Раздели её от письма:

```php
// Письмо — сразу; индекс — с задержкой, чтобы batch'ить изменения
$this->bus->dispatch(new SendWelcomeEmail($user->id));
$this->bus->dispatch(new RefreshIndex($user->id))
    ->after(10); // 10 сек — даём другим изменениям скопироваться
```

Или вообще отдельная low-priority шина:
```yaml
routing:
    'App\Domain\Event\UserWasRegistered': async
    'App\Index\Event\IndexRefresh': 'index:low'  // отдельный broker, worker с меньшим priority
```

### Trade-off
- **+** индексация не конкурирует с письмом, можно batch'ить, не гоним realtime там, где не надо.
- **–** дополнительная шина, ещё один consumer, больше движущихся частей.

---

## Вариант E: Deferred buffering (несколько действий за request)

Если за request нужно отправить *несколько* сообщений — `defer()` буферит и отправляет один раз в конце:

```php
$this->bus->dispatch($a);
$this->bus->dispatch($b);
$this->bus->defer(); // flush all at once в конце request

// или сразу:
$this->bus->dispatch(new UserWasRegistered(...), after: fn(): void => { /* flush */ });
```

**Trade-off:** экономит round-trips, но буфер держит память request'а.

---

## Сравнительная таблица

| Вариант | Async | Infra | Reliability | Для чего |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Messenger + AMQP** | ✅ | брокер | at-least-once, retry | production, нагрузка |
| **Messenger + Doctrine** | ✅ | только БД | at-least-once | small/app, dev, staging |
| **EventDispatcher inline** | ❌ | нет | — | синхронные side-эффекты |
| **Events → Messenger мост** | ✅ | брокер | как Messenger | много реакций на событие |
| **Delayed/batched index** | ✅ | доп. шина | как transport | тяжёлый non-realtime индекс |

---

## Критические нюансы (где ловятся баги)

**1. Idempotency — must-have для consumer'а.**
Messenger даёт **at-least-once**, значит письмо может уйти дважды, а индексация — дважды.
- **Письмо:** `at-most-once` через Redis-ключ с TTL (`$redis->setnx("welcome:{$id}", 1, 'EX' => 3600)`).
- **Индекс:** upsert-операции в ES/Postgres уже идемпотентны по UUID — дубли безопасны.

**2. Eventual consistency — индекса не будет мгновенно.**
Пользователь получает 201, но в индексе его может не быть несколько секунд/минут. Клиент, который сразу ищет только что зарегистрированного, увидит `404`. Это нужно либо documentировать, либо давать `Retry-After`, либо не делать поиск realtime для только что зарегистрированных.

**3. Debugging async — боль.**
Ошибки в handler'ах не падают в UI. Используй:
- `failed` transport (message уходит туда при падении),
- `debug:messenger-consume`,
- логи consumer'а, а не request'а.

**4. Ordering.**
AMQP/RabbitMQ не гарантирует порядок. Если индексация должна идти *строго после* отправки письма — либо один handler (как в примере), либо explicit ordering.

---

## Практическая рамка выбора

1. **Production под нагрузкой → Messenger + AMQP/RabbitMQ** (или Kafka, если нужен log-based stream).
2. **Small app / нет брокера → Messenger + Doctrine transport** (всё равно нужен worker).
3. **Несколько side-эффектов на событие →** Events-мост + Messenger.
4. **Индекс тяжёлый и не realtime →** выдели в отдельную delayed/low-priority шину.
5. **Критичен at-most-once (письма) →** обвязывай consumer'idempotency через Redis-key.

Самая частая ошибка — забыть про **at-least-once** и идемпотентность: в prod'е retry'и гарантированы, а дублируемое письмо или double-upsert в индекс без проверки — это реальный баг, который проявляется не в dev, а под нагрузкой.

Хотите — соберу concrete production-конфиг: `messenger.yaml` с AMQP, отдельной index-шиной, handler'ами с идемпотентностью и supervisor-конфиг для consumer'ов?

## Q07 · Symfony

**Интервьюер:** Как в Symfony устроен контроль доступа: аутентификация, роли, voters? В каких случаях выбираешь voter, а в каких достаточно ролей?

<details><summary>Ход мысли модели</summary>

The user is asking about Symfony access control: authentication, roles, voters. This is a technical PHP/Symfony question following our conversation. Let me think carefully about the accurate structure of Symfony security.

Let me recall how Symfony Security works accurately, and note version considerations since the question mentions "roles" which in newer Symfony (7.x) has a lot of changes with Attributes.

## Symfony Security Architecture - core concepts

### Authentication flow (the "authentication providers" stack)

Symfony Security has a layered architecture:

1. **Entry point** - what happens when unauthenticated user hits protected page
2. **AuthenticationManager** - orchestrates authentication
3. **Firewall** - the URL-based configuration that decides whether a request hits the security layer
4. **Authentication providers (providers)** - UserProvider loads user, and the auth provider verifies credentials
5. **Authentication success/failure handlers**

The token-based flow:
1. Request comes in
2. Firewall checks if the URL matches a protected pattern
3. Checks if there's already a valid token in the session (via the `remember`/session listener)
4. If not, it calls the authenticator(s) defined for that firewall
5. Authenticator creates an authentication token (e.g., UsernamePasswordToken)
6. AuthenticationManager authenticates it (calls the provider, verifies credentials)
7. If success, token stored in session
8. Authorization happens via AccessDecisionManager using Voters

### Authentication mechanisms

There are different authenticators:
- **Login form** (`login_form`) - handles form-based login
- **HTTP Basic** (`http_basic`)
- **API Token** (`api_token`) - bearer tokens for API
- **Token** - session-based
- **Custom authenticators**

In Symfony, the authentication layer has evolved:
- Old: `ProviderInterface` + `UserInterface` + `Encoder`
- New (Symfony 5.4+): Authenticator system with `AbstractAuthenticator` / `Interface`
- Symfony 7.2 deprecated the old system in favor of the new authenticators (Attributes-based)

### The Firewall

Each firewall has:
- URL patterns (match)
- User provider
- Authentication methods (entry point)
- Access control (rules)
- Logout
- Stateless/stateful

### UserProvider

`UserProviderInterface` - loads users:
- `loadUserByIdentit()` / `refreshUser()` (old) or `loadUser` / `saveUser` (new UserProviderInterface in Symfony 7.2)
- Built-in providers: `orm` (EntityProvider), `memory` (for dev), custom
- `UserProviderInterface::loadUserByIdentifier(string $identifier): User` and `refreshUser(User): User` in older versions

### Roles

Roles are `ROLE_*` strings. By default, Symfony's `AuthorizationChecker` checks if the user has a role.

Traditional approach:
```yaml
access_control:
  - { path: ^/admin, roles: ROLE_ADMIN }
```

In controller:
```php
$this->denyAccessUnlessGranted('ROLE_ADMIN');
```

or
```php
$this->isGranted('ROLE_ADMIN');
```

Roles get aggregated: user with `ROLE_ADMIN` also has implicit `ROLE_USER`, `ROLE_SAFF` etc. Actually the way roles work in newer Symfony:
- `ROLE_USER` is implied (you always have it if authenticated)
- Roles can be "aggregate" - if you have `ROLE_ADMIN`, you also have the "parent" roles via role hierarchy. Actually this is now done via `ROLE_ADMIN` having access to lower roles through role hierarchy in older versions.

Actually let me be precise. There are two concepts:
1. **User's actual roles** - what the user has (from `getRoles()`)
2. **Role hierarchy** - `role_hierarchy` config maps higher roles to include lower ones. E.g., `ROLE_ADMIN: [ROLE_USER, ROLE_ALLOWED_SUPERVISOR]`.

In Symfony 7.1+, the `role_hierarchy` config was deprecated in favor of using roles as "virtual"/nested directly via `#[AsRole]` or the role hierarchy being handled differently.

### AccessDecisionManager and Voters

This is the key part of the question.

**Voters** are the granular access control mechanism. A Voter is a class that:
- `isSupported(string $attribute, object $subject): bool`
- `voteOnAttribute(string $attribute, mixed $subject, TokenInterface $token): bool` (old) OR `vote(string $attribute, mixed $subject, TokenInterface $token): bool` (new Symfony 7.2+)

You implement `VoterInterface`:
```php
class ArticleVoter extends Voter {
    protected function supports(string $attribute, mixed $subject): bool {
        return str_starts_with($attribute, 'article.') && $subject instanceof Article;
    }

    protected function voteOnAttribute(string $attribute, mixed $subject, TokenInterface $token): bool {
        // ...
    }
}
```

Wait, there's a naming difference between versions:
- Older Symfony: `protected function supports(...)` returns bool, `protected function isGranted(...)` returns bool (the old Voter base class had `isGranted`)
- Symfony 7.2+: renamed to `supports()` and `vote()`. The old `Voter` class with `supports()`/`isGranted()` is deprecated.

So in current Symfony 7.2+, the base class is `AbstractVoter` (abstract) and you implement `supports()` and `vote()`.

The `AuthorizationChecker` calls the `AccessDecisionManager` (or in newer versions, the voting is done via `AuthorizationChecker::isGranted`).

**AccessDecisionManager** has three decision adapters:
1. **AffirmativeVoting** (default) - grants if ANY voter says yes
2. **ConsensusVoting** - grants if majority says yes
3. **UnanimousVoting** - requires ALL voters that support say yes (deny if ANY says no)

This is important for complex access control where you have multiple independent conditions.

### When to use Voters vs Roles

This is the core of the question. Let me think about the clear guidance:

**Use roles when:**
- Access is based on the user's assigned position/group (admin, editor, guest)
- It's about "who the user is" in terms of organizational role
- No context/subject involved
- Simple, static permission

**Use voters when:**
- Access depends on the *object/subject* being accessed (ownership: "can I edit THIS article")
- Access depends on *runtime state* (status of a resource: "can I approve this order that's in pending state")
- Access depends on complex logic combining multiple conditions
- Access depends on other services/external data

The key insight: **Roles are coarse-grained, static, identity-based. Voters are fine-grained, dynamic, context/object-based.**

A good rule of thumb: roles answer "can this user type access admin area at all?", voters answer "can this user do THIS specific action on THIS specific resource?".

Also important: you can combine them. A voter can check the user's roles AND check subject attributes.

Let me also think about the modern context with:
- `#[IsGranted(...)]` attribute on methods
- `#[AsVoter]` attribute
- `#[AsRole]` attribute (Symfony 7.1+)
- `security` annotation in attributes

Let me also mention:
- **Deny-when-attribute pattern** - voter can return `Voter::ACCESS_DENIED` / `Voter::ACCESS_ABSTAIN` / `Voter::ACCESS_GRANTED` (integer constants, new API in 7.2). This enables the DenyAttributePattern to express "deny explicitly".

Actually let me get this right. In Symfony 7.2, the `Voter` base class methods return integers:
- `Voter::ACCESS_GRANTED` (1)
- `Voter::ACCESS_DENIED` (0)
- `Voter::ACCESS_ABSTAIN` (-1)

And you extend `AbstractVoter` with generic types. The `DenyAttributePattern` trait helps implement deny-when-attribute semantics.

Let me write this. I want to be appropriately thorough but not bloated. The user has asked a fairly comprehensive question. I'll use headers and code examples, following the style established in the conversation.

Let me think about the most valuable insight for "when voter vs roles":

The deep answer: **roles are about identity and membership; voters are about context and relationships.**

Concrete signal: If the permission is the same regardless of *which specific object* you're acting on → role. If it changes based on the object's properties, ownership, or state → voter.

Also: you can have voters that check roles (voters can read `$token->getRoles()`), so voters are strictly more expressive. The question of "when to use roles" isn't "can I express it" but "should I bother."

Also worth mentioning: mixing is common - e.g., a voter that denies if user is not active, another that checks ownership. This is where UnanimousVoting matters.

Let me also mention practical gotchas:
- AccessDecisionManager default is AffirmativeVoting - a voter returning true grants access regardless of others. This means ONE permissive voter can override deny. Common surprise.
- Using Consensus or Unanimous changes semantics.
- A voter that abstains (`false` in old / `ACCESS_ABSTAIN` in new) lets other voters decide.

**Кандидат:**

## Архитектура безопасности Symfony — слои

Безопасность это конвейер из трёх слоёв:

1. **Firewall** — URL-паттерны, которые «пропускаются под защиту». Каждый firewall сам по себе: `entry_point`, провайдер пользователей, аутентикаторы, logout.
2. **Аутентификация** — аутентикаторы верифицируют учётные данные и выдают *токен*.
3. **Авторизация** — `AuthorizationChecker` через `AccessDecisionManager` и `Voter`'ы решает, можно ли действие.

Важное разделение: **аутентификация отвечает «Кто это?», авторизация — «Может ли это этот человек?».**

---

## Аутентификация

Flow (упрощённо):
1. Запрос попадает под firewall → проверяет, есть ли валидный токен в сессии/заголовке.
2. Если нет — запускает аутентикатор для этого firewall.
3. Аутентикатор создаёт *authentication token* и передаёт его в `AuthenticationManager`.
4. `AuthenticationManager` просит провайдер загрузить пользователя (`UserProviderInterface::loadUserByIdentifier`) и верифицирует учетные данные.
5. Успех → токен кешится в сессию (stateful) или возвращается в заголовке (stateless).

**Типы аутентикаторов по ситуации:**
- `login_form` — форма логина (stateful, сессия).
- `http_basic` / `api_token` — токены/API (stateless).
- `remember-me` — «запомнить меня» (длинноживущий токен).
- `custom` — свой аутентикатор.

**Эволюция API** (честно отмечу — тут часто путаница):
- **Symfony ≤ 6.3:** `ProviderInterface` + `Encoder` + старая `UserInterface` со старыми `loadUserByIdentifier()/refreshUser()`.
- **Symfony 5.4–7.1:** система аутентикаторов через `AbstractAuthenticator` (`getCredentials`, `authenticate`, `onAuthenticationSuccess`, `onAuthenticationFailure`).
- **Symfony 7.2+:** атрибутный стиль — `#[Authenticate]`, метод-хендлер, `#[WithPasswordCredentials]`, `#[ApiToken]` для API. Старая система аутентикаторов **деprecated**.

Рекомендация: не держи логику аутентификации в контроллере — выноси в кастомный аутентикатор, тогда смена способа (формы → API) локализована.

---

## Роли

Роли — это `ROLE_*`-строки, возвращаемые `getRoles()`. Три вещи, которые нужно понимать точно:

**1. `ROLE_USER` подразумевается всегда.**
Любой аутентифицированный пользователь автоматически получает `ROLE_USER`. Это не ролля, а маркер «просто аутентифицирован».

```php
public function getRoles(): array
{
    $roles = $this->roles;
    $roles[] = 'ROLE_USER'; // Symfony всё равно добавит, но часто пишут явно
    return array_unique($roles);
}
```

**2. Иерархия ролей (`role_hierarchy`) — теперь устаревает.**
Раньше ты в `security.yaml` писал:
```yaml
role_hierarchy:
  ROLE_ADMIN: [ROLE_USER, ROLE_ALLOWED_SUPERVISOR]
```
Это давало «наследование» ролей. **В Symfony 7.1+ `role_hierarchy` деprecated** — вместо него `#[AsRole]`:

```php
#[AsRole('ROLE_USER')]
#[AsRole('ROLE_ALLOWED_SUPERVISOR')]
class AdminUser implements UserInterface { /* ... */ }
```

**3. Роли — это грубая, статическая классификация «кто ты».**

```php
$this->denyAccessUnlessGranted('ROLE_ADMIN'); // или #[IsGranted('ROLE_ADMIN')]
```

---

## Voters

Voter это гранулярный контролер доступа: класс, который голосует «да/нет/воздержаться» по конкретному атрибуту и объекту.

```php
#[AsVoter]
final class ArticleVoter extends AbstractVoter
{
    public function supports(string $attribute, mixed $subject): bool
    {
        return str_starts_with($attribute, 'article.')
            && $subject instanceof Article;
    }

    public function vote(string $attribute, mixed $subject, TokenInterface $token): bool
    {
        $user = $token->getUser(); // UserInterface
        if (!$subject instanceof Article) {
            return false; // abstain — не наше
        }
        return $subject->author === $user;
    }
}
```

*(Оговорка по версиям: в Symfony ≤ 6.3 это `voteOnAttribute()` с `bool` и `Voter::isGranted()`-константы; с 7.2 — `vote()` + `Voter::ACCESS_GRANTED/ABSTAIN/DENIED` и `#[AsVoter]`. Концепция та же.)*

**AccessDecisionManager умеет голосовать тремя стратегиями** — это критично:

| Стратегия | Когда доступ granted |
|---|---|
| **Affirmative** (default) | **Хотя бы один** voter сказал «да» |
| **Consensus** | Большая часть сказали «да» |
| **Unanimous** | **Все** поддержавшие сказали «да» |

**Главный подвок:** `AffirmativeVoting` (дефолт) значит, что **один разрешающий voter переписывает все запретительные.** Если у тебя 3 voters, из которых один говорит «да», доступ даётся — даже если остальные против. Именно поэтому для «должно быть несколько условий» нужна `UnanimousVoting`.

**Deny-When-паттерн (Symfony 7.2):**
```php
use Symfony\Component\Security\Core\Authorization\Voter\DenyAttributePattern;

class OrderVoter extends AbstractVoter {
    use DenyAttributePattern; // упрощает выражение «запретить по условию»

    protected function vote(string $attribute, mixed $subject, TokenInterface $token): int {
        if ($subject->status === OrderStatus::CANCELED) {
            return Voter::ACCESS_DENIED;
        }
        return Voter::ACCESS_GRANTED;
    }
}
```

---

## Когда voter, а когда role

Сигнал простой:
- **Role** — «Кто этот пользователь» (позиция, членство в группе). Access не зависит от конкретного объекта.
- **Voter** — «Может ли этот пользователь с ЭТИМ объектом» (принадлежность, состояние, контекст).

**Практическая рамка:**

| Выбирай role, если... | Выбирай voter, если... |
|---|---|
| Доступ одинаков для всех объектов одного типа | Доступ зависит от свойств объекта |
| Проверка статична (роля не меняется в runtime) | Доступ зависит от состояния/власти/контекста |
| «Can admin enter /admin?» | «Can I edit THIS article?» |
| Пользователь принадлежит к группе | Нужно учитывать отношения «пользователь↔ресурс» |

**Рабочий принцип:** роль — про *идентичность*, voter — про *контекст и отношения*.

**Пример одного и того же разными способами:**

```php
// Role НЕ может выразить это:
// «пользователь может редактировать СВОЙ заказ»
$this->denyAccessUnlessGranted('ROLE_USER'); // слишком грубо — любой user

// Voter выражает:
#[IsGranted('article.edit')]
public function edit(Article $article): JsonResponse // $article — subject
{
    // ArticleVoter проверит, что $article->author === user
}
```

**Комбинирование (это часто лучшее решение):**
- Один voter проверяет роли (`$token->getRoles()`), второй — принадлежность. Именно здесь `UnanimousVoting` даёт смысл.
- Role может служить «быстрым фильтром», voter — «точной проверкой».

**Самые частые ошибки:**
1. **AffirmativeVoting переписывает deny.** Если хочешь «запретить при условии X», не полагайся на дефолтную стратегию — либо `Unanimous`, либо явный `ACCESS_DENIED`.
2. **Voter, который никогда не abstain'ит.** Возвращает `true` на всё подряд → ломает логику остальных.
3. **Роли вместо контекста.** `ROLE_OWNER` на каждый объект — это anti-pattern, ролли должны быть групповыми, не персонифицируемыми.
4. **Проверка доступа в шаблоне без защиты.** `isGranted()` в Twig — это UI-фикс, но **реальную проверку надо делать в контроллере/сервисе**, иначе API обходят проверки шаблона.
5. **`getRoles()` из БД без `ROLE_USER`.** Хотя Symfony добавляет его — на всякий можно не полагаться в строго типизированном коде, лучше явно.

---

## Краткий вывод

Roles и voters не конкурируют — они разного уровня абстракции. **Role = «ты админ» (грубо, статично, про группy). Voter = «ты можешь сделать это именно с этим объектом» (гранулярно, динамично, про контекст).**

Хорошая практика: role закрывает coarse-grained границы (раздел для админов), voter — тонкий доступ к конкретным ресурсам (свой заказ, активная заявка). И не забудь про стратегию голосования — `Affirmative` (дефолт) опасна, когда тебе нужен строгий «все за».

Хотите — разберу конкретный кейс, например «пользователь может редактировать заказы только в статусе pending» или «команда редакторов vs владелец статьи», и покажу, как выразить его ролями и как — voters'ами, с выбором стратегии голосования?

Let me think about what I actually know here and what the honest picture is.

**The core problem:** Value Objects (VOs) in DDD are defined by their attributes, not identity. They're immutable and invariant-guaranteed. But Doctrine's fundamental unit of persistence is the *Entity*, which has identity and is mutable by design. So there's a conceptual mismatch we need to bridge.

**The main mapping strategies in Doctrine:**

1. **EmbeddedObjects / Embeddable** — this is the canonical Doctrine answer.
   - `#[Embedded]` attribute (Doctrine ORM 2.9+, was `@Embeddable`/`@Embedded` JPA annotations)
   - An embedded class has no identity of its own; its fields become columns in the owning entity's table.
   - Doctrine creates the VO on the fly by reading columns.

2. **Custom Type (Doctrine Types / Custom Mapping)** — `AbstractDoctrineType` or `CustomType`.
   - Store as a single column, usually serialized (JSON, serialized PHP, or custom format).
   - The VO is fully opaque to the ORM — one column holds the whole value.

3. **ValueObject as embedded columns** — store individual attributes as columns manually.

Let me think about the trade-offs and pitfalls of each.

**EmbeddedObjects (#[Embedded]):**

Pros:
- Each attribute of the VO maps to its own column → you get SQL querying/filtering on individual attributes.
- The VO is still a proper domain concept.
- Doctrine handles the mapping.

Cons and pitfalls:
- **Immutability conflict**: Doctrine's hydration uses setters to build objects (or reflection). If your VO is immutable with only a constructor, Doctrine historically had trouble. Actually, in some versions, it can use the constructor if the class has a constructor matching the field order, but it's finicky.
- **Always-valid invariant**: With embedded, the VO is constructed from columns, bypassing your validation. So if a column is invalid (torn data), Doctrine might give you an invalid VO. Need to guard.
- **Table explosion / denormalization**: Each embedded VO's columns live in the parent table.

**Custom Type (single column):**

Pros:
- Clean separation — VO is opaque, domain owns representation.
- Immutability preserved — you control deserialization in the type.
- Single column, simpler table.
- Can serialize as JSON, which is flexible.

Cons and pitfalls:
- **No SQL querying** on the VO's internals (unless using JSON columns with PostgreSQL). This is a big one — you can't do `WHERE` on individual fields.
- **Serialization format coupling** — JSON bloat, versioning issues.
- **PostgreSQL JSONB** can partially solve querying.
- **Validation**: the custom type's `convertToDatabaseValue`/`convertToPHPValue` is where deserialization happens. If you make the VO always-valid via constructor, and the DB has bad data, the type conversion throws → this surfaces at read time.
- The custom type is a great place to enforce invariants on load.

There's also the consideration of **read models** — many teams avoid Doctrine hydrating immutable VOs and instead use a custom approach.

Let me also think about:

- **Array of VOs** — e.g. storing list of address embedded via `#[Embedded]` or JSONB array.

**Invariance enforcement strategies:**

1. Make the VO's constructor validate (private constructor returning result type) → always-valid. But then deserialization needs to either trust it or wrap it.
2. Use a builder/factory.
3. Consider that Doctrine hydration bypasses the constructor in some cases → invariants not enforced. So you need a validation layer on load, or rely on the Custom Type to enforce.

**Best practices / recommendations:**

- For **queryable VOs**: use `#[Embedded]` or individual columns, but accept that Doctrine hydration may need setters (trade immutability in the mapping or use constructor carefully). Alternatively, split: immutable VO in domain, but map attributes as columns.
- For **opaque VOs**: Custom Type with JSON/serialized, single column.

## Главный конфликт

Doctrine построена вокруг Entity — **объекта с идентичностью и мутабельностью по дизайну** (гидратор Doctrine использует reflection + сеттеры). Value Object по определению **без идентичности и иммутабельный**. То есть Doctrine не умеет «просто взять» иммутабельный объект — ты всегда мостишь пропасть между этими моделями. Отсюда все trade-offs.

Три рабочих стратегии маппинга:

1. `#[Embedded]` — каждый атрибут VOs = свой столбец.
2. Custom Type (свой Doctrine-тип) — весь VOs = один непрозрачный столбец.
3. JSONB (PostgreSQL) — VOs как JSON с возможностью запроса.

---

## Способ 1: `#[Embedded]` (Embeddable)

Атрибут в домене «растворяется» в столбцы родительской сущности:

```php
#[Embeddable]
final class Money {
    public function __construct(
        public readonly int $amount,     // в центах
        public readonly string $currency,
    ) {}
}

final class Invoice {
    #[Embedded]
    private Money $total;

    #[Embedded]
    private Money $subtotal;

    #[Column(type: 'decimal')]
    private ?float $tax = null;
}
```

Опции: `#[Embedded(class: '...')]`, `#[Embedded(name: '...')]` для переименования столбцов.

### Trade-offs
- **+** Каждый атрибут → отдельный столбец → можно строить запросы в SQL (`WHERE total_amount > 100`).
- **+** VOs остаётся доменным concept, а не blob.
- **–** **Conflict с иммутабельностью** (см. ↓).
- **–** Деномализация: таблица разбухает, поля VOs живут в чужой таблице.

### Подводный камень: гидратация vs иммутабельность
Doctrine гидратирует Embeddable-объект через **reflection + сеттеры**, а не через конструктор (в большинстве версий). С иммутабельным `readonly` классом Doctrine **не сможет заполнить поля** — у тебя либо сеттеры (рушение immutability в слое маппинга), либо придётся полагаться на constructor-based hydration, которая работает не стабильно. Это **главная боль** стратегии.

---

## Способ 2: Custom Type (AbstractType)

VO остаётся opaque для Doctrine — **один столбец, и ты сам управляешь сериализацией**.

```php
final class MoneyType extends AbstractType
{
    public function getColumnType(): string
    {
        return 'json'; // или bigint для «cents + currency» парой столбцов
    }

    public function convertToDatabaseValue(
        mixed $value,
        ?AbstractSchemaManager $schemaManager,
    ): mixed {
        return $value !== null ? $value->encode() : null;
    }

    public function convertToPHPValue(
        mixed $value,
        ?AbstractSchemaManager $schemaManager,
    ): mixed {
        // Here you enforce invariants on load
        return $value !== null ? Money::decode($value) : null;
    }
}
```

```yaml
# config.yaml
doctrine:
  dbal:
    types:
      money: App\Doctrine\Types\MoneyType
```

```php
final class Invoice {
    #[Column(type: 'money')]
    private Money $total;
}
```

*(Оговорка: подпись `convertToPHPValue`/`convertToDatabaseValue` менялась в DBAL 4 — `AbstractSchemaManager` стал nullable; версия имеет значение.)*

### Trade-offs
- **+** Иммутабельность **полностью сохранена** — ты де сериализуешь через `Money::decode()`, контролируешь валидацию.
- **+** VO не «размазан» по таблицам, домен владеет представлением.
- **–** **Нет SQL-запросов** по атрибутам VOs (один столбец). Это крупнейшее ограничение.
- **–** Формат сериализации — зона риска: бloat JSON, версионирование, `unserialize`-опасности.
- **–** Точка валидации — на этапе чтения, так что «битые» данные из DB всплывут в `convertToPHPValue`.

### Инварианты как плюс этой стратегии
Because you own `convertToPHPValue`, это **естественное место для enforcement**:

```php
public function Money::decode(string $data): self
{
    [$cents, $currency] = json_decode($data, true, flags: JSON_THROW_ON_ERROR);
    // Если валидация в private-конструкторе/фабрике — тут поднимется ошибка
    return self::fromCents($cents)->to($currency);
}
```

Такой подход «always-valid» гарантирует, что ты никогда не получишь валидное по имени, но битое по данным VOs.

---

## Способ 3: JSONB в PostgreSQL

Смесь обоих подходов — VO в одном столбце, но Postgres позволяет **частичный запрос**:

```php
final class Invoice {
    #[Column(type: 'json')]   // Postgres → jsonb
    private Money $total;
}
```

```php
// Твой Custom Type сериализует в/из JSON
// Запрос по вложенному полю:
$em->getRepository(Invoice::class)
  ->createQueryBuilder('i')
  ->addSelect('CAST(i.total AS jsonb)')
  ->where('CAST(i.total AS jsonb) ->> \'currency\' = :curr')
  ->setParameter('curr', 'USD');
```

### Trade-offs
- **+** Запросы к внутренностям VOs через операторы JSONB.
- **+** Иммутабельность сохраняется (через Custom Type).
- **+** Гибкость — VOs можно менять без миграции схемы.
- **–** Bind к Postgres (теряешь portability).
- **–** JSONB-запросы медленнее, чем по индексированным столбцам; индексы (GIN) занимают место.
- **–** Мелкие вложенные поля не индексируются так же чисто, как column.

---

## Сравнительная таблица

| Критерий | `#[Embedded]` | Custom Type | JSONB (Postgres) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Запрос в SQL | ✅ (столбцы) | ❌ (один столбец) | ✅ частично (операторы) |
| Иммутабельность | ⚠️ конфликт гидрации | ✅ ты контролируешь | ✅ ты контролируешь |
| Инварианты | ⚠️ обходятся на гидрации | ✅ на чтении | ✅ на чтении |
| Размер/схема | много столбцов, деномализация | 1 столбец | 1 крупный столбец |
| Зависимости | Doctrine ORM | Doctrine DBAL | Postgres |
| Коллекции VOs | ⚠️ капризно | ✅ (массив в столбце) | ✅ |

---

## Коллекции Value Objects — отдельная боль

Doctrine плохо относится к коллекциям Embeddable:
- `#[Embedded]` внутри `ArrayCollection` ведёт себя нестабильно.
- Чаще для «массив VOs» берут **Custom Type + JSONB-массив** или `json`-столбец с сериализацией массива.

```php
final class AddressListType extends AbstractType {
    // toArray(Address[]) → json
    // toArray(json) → Address[]  (каждый Address — immutable VOs)
}
```

---

## Практическая рамка выбора

1. **Нужны SQL-запросы по атрибутам VOs → `#[Embedded]`**, но готовься: иммутабельность в слое маппинга будет конфликтовать с гидрацией Doctrine. Решение — либо mutable-маппинг, либо вынос логики.

2. **Immutability и инварианты важнее запросов → Custom Type.** Это чистый компромисс: домен владеет представлением, валидация на этапе чтения.

3. **PostgreSQL + нужен и immutability, и запросы → JSONB + Custom Type.** Лучшее из обоих миров, но привязка к Postgres.

4. **Не заставляй Doctrine гидрировать иммутабельные VOs напрямую** — если `#[Embedded]` ломает immutability, это сигнал, что Doctrine's hydration model противоречит твоему домену, и Custom Type здесь честнее.

---

## Честные оговорки и подводные камни

- **Doctrine's hydration model предполагает мутабельные объекты** с сеттерами — это архитектурная несовместимость с.Immutable VOs, а не баг. Custom Type — способ её обойти, не борясь с движком.
- **Инварианты на гидрации `#[Embedded]` обходятся**, потому что Doctrine не вызывает твой валидирующий конструктор. Если «always-valid» критичен — `#[Embedded]` влечёт риск битого состояния.
- **Custom Type-сериализация** — зона уязвимости: JSON предпочтительнее PHP-`unserialize()` (это потенциальный RCE).
- **Версионирование формата** — если VOs поменяет структуру, старые строки в DB сломают `convertToPHPValue`. Держи деградацию/миграцию.
- **Null VOs** — Doctrine гидрит `null` как `null`, не как «empty VO»; проверь, что твоя доменная логика это обрабатывает, иначе получишь unexpected null.
- **Тестирование Custom Type** — не забывай юнит-тестировать round-trip `convertToDatabaseValue ↔ convertToPHPValue`, иначе баг всплывёт только при работе с БД.

---

## Краткий вывод

Выбор между стратегиями — это выбор между **запрашиваемостью** (Embeddable, JSONB) и **чистотой домена** (Custom Type). Для VOs, где иммутабельность и валидация — ядро концепции, **Custom Type (или JSONB в Postgres) обычно честнее `#[Embedded]`**, потому что Doctrine's модель гидрации по определению не умеет иммутабельные объекты, а `#[Embedded]` заставит тебя либо отказаться от immutability в маппинге, либо потерять валидацию.

Хотите — разберу конкретный кейс: например, «команда заказов» или «адрес доставки VOs в коллекции», и покажу три пути его хранения (Embeddable / Custom Type / JSONB) с реальной схемой, миграцией и запросами в каждом случае?

Let me think about the trickiest parts that people get wrong:

**Problem 1: The owning side of the relationship**
- In `@OneToMany`, the One side is the *inverse* side (not owning)
- In `@ManyToOne`, the Many side is the *owning* side (has the join column)
- So to persist a child, you must set the reference on the child (Many side), not just add to collection on the parent

**Problem 2: Cascade and orphanRemoval**
- `cascade={"persist"}` — children are persisted automatically when parent is persisted
- `cascade={"remove"}` — removing parent removes children
- `orphanRemoval=true` — if child removed from collection, it's deleted (requires cascade=remove)

**Problem 3: Aggregate semantics vs Doctrine**
- Doctrine has no aggregate concept. If you have `Order` and separately load `LineItem` without the Order, Doctrine treats them as independent. This breaks aggregate invariants.
- Children should NOT be loadable/manipulated independently.

**Problem 4: Transaction boundaries**
- One transaction per Aggregate Root operation (per aggregate root command)
- The Unit of Work ensures atomicity: all changes to one aggregate flushed atomically
- If multiple aggregates change, you have multiple UoWs to coordinate

**Problem 5: Who calls flush**
- Repository calls flush in its method (save/create)
- But careful: `persist` then `flush` — flush flushes everything in the Unit of Work
- Don't flush too eagerly (multiple flushes = multiple transactions)
- Don't flush in service for every entity

Let me think about a subtle but important point: **the difference between persist() and flush() and how Doctrine's Unit of Work works.**

Doctrine's persistence is lazy (by default). You don't need to call `persist()` on every child if it's cascaded — but you DO need to call `persist($aggregateRoot)` to bring it into the identity map, then `flush()` to commit the whole Unit of Work.

Actually a very important nuance: **the Unit of Work (UoW) is per-EntityManager.** So all changes are tracked and flushed atomically within one UoW. If you want multiple aggregates in one transaction, they share the same UoW (same EM), so one flush commits them together.

But there's a subtlety: if two aggregates are modified, one flush commits both — that could be a cross-aggregate transaction, which is often undesirable (violates aggregate boundaries). In strict DDD you'd want each aggregate transaction independent. This is the DDD-vs-Doctrine tension.

```php
#[ORM\Entity(repositoryClass: OrderRepository::class)]

    private string $id;

    private string $status = OrderStatus::CREATED;

    #[ORM\OneToMany(
        targetEntity: LineItem::class,
        mappedBy: 'order',
        cascade: ['persist', 'remove'],
        orphanRemoval: true,
    )]
    /** @var LineItem[] */
    private ArrayCollection $lineItems;

        targetEntity: Document::class,
        cascade: ['persist'],
        orphanRemoval: true,
    )]

    private array $documents;

    public function __construct()
    {
        $this->lineItems = new ArrayCollection();
        $this->documents = new array();
    }

    // Aggregate methods (state transitions)
    public function addLineItem(LineItem $item): void
    {
        $item->setOrder($this); // ← owning side!
        $this->lineItems->add($item);
    }

    public function removeLineItem(LineItem $item): void
    {
        $this->lineItems->removeElement($item);
        // orphanRemoval handles deletion
    }

    public function confirm(): void
    {
        if ($this->lineItems->isEmpty()) {
            throw new InvalidStateException('Order must have line items to confirm');
        }
        // invariant check
        $this->status = OrderStatus::CONFIRMED;
    }
}
```

```php
$this->em->beginTransaction();

    $this->em->flush();

} catch (\Throwable $e) {

}
```

**Cross-aggregate problem**: This is a great point to make. Doctrine's UoW is per-EntityManager and flushes ALL managed changes atomically. In strict DDD, aggregates should be transactionally independent (you wouldn't want to commit Order B if Order A fails, or they might be in different databases in a microservices setup). Doctrine doesn't model this. So the pattern is: each aggregate operation = one repository call = one transaction, and if you need two, you coordinate manually.

Let me reconsider the transaction part for accuracy:

Doctrine `EntityManager`:
- By default, the underlying PDO driver may auto-commit each statement.
- `flush()` performs the INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE via the Unit of Work.
- Without an explicit transaction, statements may be auto-committed individually → NOT atomic.
- To ensure atomicity: `beginTransaction()` before operations, `flush()`, then `commit()`.
- `rollback()` on error.

In Symfony 6.3+/Doctrine 2.15+, `#[AsTransactional]` from `Doctrine\Bundle\DoctrineBundle\Attribute\AsTransactional` is the clean way:
```php
use Doctrine\DoctrineBundle\Attribute\AsTransactional;

#[AsTransactional]
public function save(Order $order): void { ... }
```
This wraps the method in a transaction. Note: only one `#[AsTransactional]` is allowed per method (nested ones throw).

Also there's the option of using a command bus / saga pattern.

Let me mention that in the default (non-explicit) case, if you just call persist + flush without beginTransaction, each statement auto-commits. So atomicity is NOT free.

Also, there's a subtlety with `flush()` and the UoW being per-EntityManager: all managed entities are in one UoW, so one flush = one batch of statements. Whether that's wrapped in one transaction depends on whether you explicitly began one.

---

## Mental model: Doctrine doesn't know about aggregates

The first thing to accept: **Doctrine has no notion of an aggregate root.** It sees three independent entities — `Order`, `LineItem`, `Document` — connected by FK columns. Aggregate semantics (invariants, transactional isolation) are enforced by *you*, not by Doctrine. Doctrine's unit of work is the closest thing to atomicity, and it's per-EntityManager.

This mismatch is where most mistakes come from:
- Children loadable independently (violating invariants).
- Cross-aggregate operations committed together.
- Setting the inverse side of a relationship expecting it to persist.

---

## Example: Order → LineItem → Document (two levels)

### Mapping

```php
#[ORM\Entity(repositoryClass: OrderRepository::class)]
final class Order
{
    #[ORM\Id]
    #[ORM\Column(type: 'uuid')]
    private string $id;

    #[ORM\OneToMany(
        targetEntity: LineItem::class,
        mappedBy: 'order',
        cascade: ['persist', 'remove'],
        orphanRemoval: true,
    )]
    private Collection $lineItems;

    public function __construct()
    {
        $this->lineItems = new ArrayCollection();
    }

    // All mutations go through aggregate methods → invariants held
    public function addLineItem(LineItem $item): void
    {
        $item->setOrder($this);          // ← OWNING side must be set
        $this->lineItems->add($item);    // collection
    }

    public function removeLineItem(LineItem $item): void
    {
        $this->lineItems->removeElement($item);  // orphanRemoval → DELETE at flush
    }

    public function confirm(): void
    {
        if ($this->lineItems->isEmpty()) {
            throw new IllegalStateException('Cannot confirm an empty order');
        }
    }
}
```

```php
#[ORM\Entity]
final class LineItem
{
    #[ORM\Id]
    #[ORM\Column(type: 'uuid')]
    private string $id;

    // OWNING side of the many-to-one: owns the join column
    #[ORM\ManyToOne(targetEntity: Order::class, inversedBy: 'lineItems')]
    #[ORM\JoinColumn(nullable: false)]
    private ?Order $order = null;

    public function setOrder(Order $order): void
    {
        $this->order = $order;
    }

    // Grandchildren: same pattern one level down
    #[ORM\OneToMany(
        targetEntity: Document::class,
        mappedBy: 'lineItem',
        cascade: ['persist', 'remove'],
        orphanRemoval: true,
    )]
    private Collection $documents;
}
```

```php
#[ORM\Entity]
final class Document
{
    #[ORM\Id]
    #[ORM\Column(type: 'uuid')]
    private string $id;

    // OWNING side of lineItem.documents
    #[ORM\ManyToOne(targetEntity: LineItem::class, inversedBy: 'documents')]
    #[ORM\JoinColumn(nullable: false)]
    private ?LineItem $lineItem = null;

    public function setLineItem(LineItem $lineItem): void
    {
        $this->lineItem = $lineItem;
    }
}
```

### Owning vs inverse side — the #1 pitfall

- ManyToOne = **owning side** (holds the FK / join column).
- OneToMany(mappedBy=...) = **inverse side** (read-only metadata, no FK).

Doctrine writes the join column **only from the owning side**. So in `addLineItem` you MUST call `$item->setOrder($this)` — just adding to `$order->lineItems` will persist the item (via cascade) but leave `order_id` NULL. Setting both sides is the safe habit; setting the owning side is the minimum.

### cascade vs orphanRemoval

- `cascade: ['persist']` — persisting the parent persists children in the collection (recursively — so persisting an `Order` persists `LineItem` and its `Document`).
- `cascade: ['remove']` — removing the parent removes children.
- `orphanRemoval: true` — removing a child *from the parent's collection* issues a DELETE. Requires `cascade: ['remove']` (otherwise ignored).

`orphanRemoval` is the ORM-level deletion that makes `removeElement` (not `remove()`) work the way you'd expect for aggregate children.

### Two more notes on mapping

- Add a DB-level `onDelete: 'CASCADE'` on `JoinColumn` for referential integrity; but be aware ORM-level `orphanRemoval` and DB cascade can fight if a child is removed through different paths.
- If you don't want a child to be independently addressable (aggregates), **don't expose its repository** and treat its id as internal. Otherwise Doctrine will happily load a `LineItem` and let you mutate it outside the `Order` — silently breaking the invariant.

---

## Transaction boundaries

Doctrine tracks managed entities in the **Unit of Work** (per `EntityManager`). `flush()` writes all pending changes. But:

- **By default, Doctrine does NOT wrap `flush()` in a transaction** — each statement can auto-commit depending on the driver. So a persist + flush of one order with children is **not** all-or-nothing unless you begin a transaction.
- **Atomicity boundary = one transaction per aggregate operation.**

Cleanest way (Symfony → Doctrine 2.15+):

```php
use Doctrine\Bundle\DoctrineBundle\Attribute\AsTransactional;

class OrderRepository
{
    #[AsTransactional]
    public function save(Order $order): void
    {
        $this->em->persist($order);  // cascade persists LineItem + Document
        $this->em->flush();
    }
}
```

or explicit:

```php
public function save(Order $order): void
{
    $this->em->beginTransaction();
    try {
        $this->em->persist($order);
        $this->em->flush();
        $this->em->commit();
    } catch (\Throwable $e) {
        $this->em->rollBack();
        throw $e;
    }
}
```

`#[AsTransactional]` — only one per method (nested ones throw). Keep the transaction short: no HTTP calls, no heavy compute inside.

### The cross-aggregate problem

Doctrine's `flush()` commits **all** managed changes atomically. If you modify two orders in one service call, one flush commits both — together, in one transaction. In strict DDD you'd want each aggregate transactionally independent. Doctrine doesn't model that. So:
- Prefer one repository call = one aggregate = one transaction.
- If you truly need multiple, either accept the shared transaction (document it) or coordinate transactions yourself.

---

## Who and when flush

- **Who:** the **Repository** (Data Mapper). Entities must not call `flush()` themselves, and services shouldn't persist directly. The repository owns persistence.
- **When:** **once per aggregate operation**, inside the transaction. Never flush in a loop, never per child.
- **Don't persist children explicitly** — `persist($order)` cascade-persists them. You only persist the root.

Repository:

```php
final class OrderRepository
{
    public function __construct(private EntityManagerInterface $em) {}

    #[AsTransactional]
    public function save(Order $order): void
    {
        $this->em->persist($order);
        $this->em->flush();
    }

    public function load(string $id): Order
    {
        return $this->em->getRepository(Order::class)->find($id);
    }
}
```

```php
class OrderCommandHandler
{
    public function __construct(private OrderRepository $orders) {}

    public function confirm(string $id): void
    {
        $order = $this->orders->load($id);  // whole aggregate + children
        $order->confirm();                  // invariant-checked
        $this->orders->save($order);        // one transaction
    }
}
```

### The lazy collection cost

Children load lazily — the first access to `$order->lineItems` triggers a query. In a loop, that's N+1. Fix with `JOIN FETCH` in DQL:

```php
$this->em->createQuery('SELECT o, li FROM Order o LEFT JOIN o.lineItems li WHERE o.id = :id')
    ->setParameter('id', $id)
    ->getSingleResult();
```

---

## Mental model: Doctrine doesn't know about aggregates

The first thing to accept: **Doctrine has no notion of an aggregate root.** It sees three independent entities — `Order`, `LineItem`, `Document` — linked by FK columns. Aggregate semantics (invariants, transactional isolation) are enforced by *you*, not by Doctrine. The unit of work is the closest Doctrine gets to atomicity, and it's **per `EntityManager`.**

This mismatch is where every mistake comes from:
- Children loadable independently → invariants violated.
- Cross-aggregate operations committed together.
- Setting the *inverse* side of a relationship and expecting it to persist.

Let me use a two-level example — **Order → LineItem → Document** — because nested collections are the hardest Doctrine case and surface every pitfall.

## Mapping collections

### The three entities

```php
#[ORM\Entity(repositoryClass: OrderRepository::class)]
final class Order
{
    #[ORM\Id]
    #[ORM\Column(type: 'uuid')]
    private string $id;

    #[ORM\OneToMany(
        targetEntity: LineItem::class,
        mappedBy: 'order',
        cascade: ['persist', 'remove'],
        orphanRemoval: true,
    )]
    private Collection $lineItems;

    public function __construct()
    {
        $this->lineItems = new ArrayCollection();
    }

    // All mutations go through aggregate methods → invariants held
    public function addLineItem(LineItem $item): void
    {
        $item->setOrder($this);   // ← OWNING side must be set!
        $this->lineItems->add($item);
    }

    public function removeLineItem(LineItem $item): void
    {
        $this->lineItems->removeElement($item);   // orphanRemoval → DELETE at flush
    }

    public function confirm(): void
    {
        if ($this->lineItems->isEmpty()) {
            throw new IllegalStateException('Cannot confirm an empty order');
        }
    }
}
```

```php
#[ORM\Entity]
final class LineItem
{
    #[ORM\Id]
    #[ORM\Column(type: 'uuid')]
    private string $id;

    // OWNING side of the many-to-one: owns the join column
    #[ORM\ManyToOne(targetEntity: Order::class, invedBy: 'lineItems')]
    #[ORM\JoinColumn(nullable: false)]
    private ?Order $order = null;

    public function setOrder(Order $order): void
    {
        $this->order = $order;
    }

    // Grandchildren: same pattern one level down
    #[ORM\OneToMany(
        targetEntity: Document::class,
        mappedBy: 'lineItem',
        cascade: ['persist', 'remove'],
        orphanRemoval: true,
    )]
    private Collection $documents;
}
```

```php
#[ORM\Entity]
final class Document
{
    #[ORM\Id]
    #[ORM\Column(type: 'uuid')]
    private string $id;

    // OWNING side of lineItem.documents
    #[ORM\ManyToOne(targetEntity: LineItem::class, inversedBy: 'documents')]
    #[ORM\JoinColumn(nullable: false)]
    private ?LineItem $lineItem = null;

    public function setLineItem(LineItem $lineItem): void
    {
        $this->lineItem = $lineItem;
    }
}
```

### Owning vs inverse side — the #1 pitfall

- **ManyToOne = owning side** (holds the FK / join column).
- **OneToMany `mappedBy=...` = inverse side** (read-only metadata, no FK).

Doctrine writes the join column **only from the owning side.** So in `addLineItem` you must call `$item->setOrder($this)` — adding just to `$order->lineItems` will persist the item (via cascade) but leave `order_id` NULL. Setting both sides is the safe habit; setting the owning side is the minimum.

### cascade vs orphanRemoval

- `cascade: ['persist']` — persisting the parent persists the children in the collection **recursively** (persisting an `Order` persists `LineItem` *and* its `Document`).
- `cascade: ['remove']` — removing the parent removes children.
- `orphanRemoval: true` — removing a child **from the parent's collection** issues a DELETE. Requires `cascade: ['remove']` (otherwise silently ignored).

`orphanRemoval` is the ORM-level deletion that makes `removeElement()` (not `remove()`) behave the way you'd expect for aggregate children.

### Two mapping notes

- Add a DB-level `onDelete: 'CASCADE'` on `JoinColumn` for referential integrity — but be aware ORM-level `orphanRemoval` and DB cascade can fight if a child is removed through different paths.
- Don't expose a child's repository, and treat its id as internal — otherwise Doctrine will happily load a `LineItem` and let you mutate it outside the `Order`, silently breaking the invariant.

## Transaction boundaries

Doctrine tracks managed entities in the **Unit of Work** (per `EntityManager`). `flush()` writes all pending changes — but:

- **By default, Doctrine does NOT wrap `flush()` in a transaction** — depending on the driver, each statement can auto-commit. So a persist+flush of one order with children is **not** all-or-nothing unless you begin a transaction.
- **Atomicity boundary = one transaction per aggregate operation.**

Cleanest way (Symfony → Doctrine 2.15+):

```php
use Doctrine\Bundle\DoctrineBundle\Attribute\AsTransactional;

class OrderRepository
{
    #[AsTransactional]
    public function save(Order $order): void
    {
        $this->em->persist($order);   // cascade persists LineItem + Document
        $this->em->flush();
    }
}
```

or explicit begin/commit/rollback. Note: only one `#[AsTransactional]` per method (nested ones throw), and keep the transaction short — no HTTP calls or heavy compute inside.

### The cross-aggregate problem

Doctrine's `flush()` commits **all** managed changes atomically. If you modify two orders in one service call, one flush commits both together — in one transaction. In strict DDD you'd want each aggregate transactionally independent; Doctrine doesn't model that. So:

- Prefer one repository call = one aggregate = one transaction.
- If you truly need multiple, either accept the shared transaction (document it) or coordinate transactions yourself.

## Who and when flush

- **Who:** the **Repository** (Data Mapper). Entities must not call `flush()` themselves, and services shouldn't persist directly. Persistence is the repository's job.
- **When:** **once per aggregate operation**, inside the transaction. Never flush in a loop, never per child.
- **Don't persist children explicitly** — `persist($order)` cascade-persists them. You only persist the root.

```php
final class OrderRepository
{
    public function __construct(private EntityManagerInterface $em) {}

    #[AsTransactional]
    public function save(Order $order): void
    {
        $this->em->persist($order);
        $this->em->flush();
    }

    public function load(string $id): Order
    {
        return $this->em->getRepository(Order::class)->find($id);
    }
}
```

```php
class OrderCommandHandler
{
    public function __construct(private OrderRepository $orders) {}

    public function confirm(string $id): void
    {
        $order = $this->orders->load($id);  // whole aggregate + children
        $order->confirm();                  // invariant-checked
        $this->orders->save($order);        // one transaction
    }
}
```

### The lazy collection cost

Children load lazily — the first access to `$order->lineItems` triggers a query. In a loop, that's N+1. Fix with `JOIN FETCH` in DQL:

```php
$this->em->createQuery(
    'SELECT o, li FROM Order o LEFT JOIN o.lineItems li WHERE o.id = :id'
)->setParameter('id', $id)->getSingleResult();
```

## Pitfalls to remember

1. **Setting the inverse side only** → join column stays NULL.
2. **Persisting children explicitly** → redundant; cascade handles it.
3. **`orphanRemoval` without `cascade: remove`** → silently ignored.
4. **Loading children independently** → invariants broken; don't expose child repositories.
5. **Flushing in a loop** → many transactions, slow.
6. **Assuming flush is atomic** → it isn't; manage the transaction explicitly.
7. **Modifying two aggregates in one handler** → they commit together; that's usually wrong.

The short version: mapping is mechanical (owning side + cascade + orphanRemoval), but the aggregate is enforced by *you* — mutation through aggregate methods for invariants, one repository call per aggregate for atomicity, and explicit transactions because Doctrine doesn't give you atomicity for free.

5. `--show-queries`
6. Profiler queries

For N+1 specifically:
- `SELECT * FROM order WHERE id = ?` then `SELECT * FROM line_item WHERE order_id = ?` in a loop = classic N+1

Solutions:
1. `LEFT JOIN FETCH` in DQL — fetch eagerly
2. `JOIN FETCH` in Query Builder
3. `@ORM\ManyToMany` with fetch=EXTRA
4. Custom repository methods with query builder
5. `select` clauses in DQL to also fetch associations
6. For collections, you can't use mappedBy fetch easily — use query builder
7. `JOIN FETCH` has limitations: can only eagerly fetch associations that are part of the same query, cannot fetch many-to-one and collection in same query if they conflict, no pagination with fetch (count issue)

Let me think about trade-offs:

**JOIN FETCH (DQL/QueryBuilder):**
- Pros: single query, no lazy loading
- Cons: Cartesian product explosion with multiple associations, no pagination with COUNT, can only fetch one level effectively, no partial objects, doesn't work when you need different queries

Actually there's nuance: In newer Doctrine, you CAN fetch multiple associations but it causes cartesian products. There's a known limitation: you can only use LEFT JOIN FETCH with one association (the other becomes non-fetch). Actually, Doctrine 2.9+ you can use multiple LEFT JOIN FETCH.

**EAGER fetch (fetch="EXTRA"):**
- Pros: automatic, no code change to queries
- Cons: harder to reason about, can produce cartesian products, doesn't help with paginated queries

**Custom repository with group_by / partial objects:**
- For pagination, `GROUP BY entity.id` to deduplicate

**DTO projection:**
- Select only what you need — `->select('partial o.{id, name}, partial li.{id, amount}')` or DTOs
- Pros: minimal data transfer
- Cons: two-phase, loses entity identity, partial objects

**Second level cache:**
- For entities that don't change often

**Query result cache:**
- Doctrine Query Cache, Result Cache

Let me think about the pagination problem specifically — this is a real gotcha:
- `JOIN FETCH` + pagination → the COUNT query counts rows before dedup, so COUNT is wrong
- Solution: `SELECT o FROM Order o LEFT JOIN o.lineItems li` then in `setFirstResult`/`setMaxResult`, use GROUP BY o.id in count query, or fetch IDs first then fetch.

**Кандидат:**

## Diagnostic first — prove it's N+1, don't guess

N+1 looks like other latency problems, so verify before optimizing:

**1. WebProfiler SQL panel (dev):** open any page, look at the "SQL" tab. Count queries. N+1 shows up as **the same SQL statement repeated many times with only the bound parameters changing** — e.g. one `SELECT * FROM order` then 50× `SELECT * FROM line_item WHERE order_id = ?` with different values.

```bash
bin/console debug:profiler --show-queries --format=txt
```

If you see N near-identical queries differing only by bind params, that's your N+1.

**2. Doctrine SQL logger (precise):**
```php
$em->getConnection()->getConfiguration()->setSQLLogger(
    new class extends \Doctrine\DBAL\Logging\DebugSQLLogger {
        public function startQuery(): void { /* record timing */ }
    }
);
```
In dev you can also enable the profiler's data collector automatically.

**3. APM (production):** Blackfire flame graph or New Relic highlights exactly which loop iteration runs the queries. Blackfire is great here because it shows the N identical queries stacked in the function's execution time.

**4. The smoking-gun signal:** if query count scales with rows returned (fetching 50 orders → 51 queries), it's N+1. If it's a fixed 10 queries regardless of row count, it's something else (maybe N+1 on associations, maybe joinable tables).

## The classic case

```php
foreach ($orders as $order) {                 // 1 query
    foreach ($order->getLineItems() as $item) {   // N queries ← the problem
        echo $item->getAmount();               // another lazy query often
    }
}
```

The root cause: Doctrine loads `Order` eagerly but its `lineItems` collection is **lazy by default** — every iteration triggers a fresh `SELECT`.

## Solutions and trade-offs

### 1. `LEFT JOIN FETCH` in DQL — the canonical fix

```php
$orders = $em->createQuery(
    'SELECT o, li FROM Order o LEFT JOIN o.lineItems li WHERE o.id = :id'
)->setParameter('id', $id)->getResult();
```

**Pros:** single query, no lazy loading, collections fully populated.
**Cons:** 
- **Cartesian product explosion** — if you also fetch another association (e.g. customer), you get `orders × lineItems × customers` rows.
- **You can't use `em->find()`** for eager collection loading — find() always returns lazy collections. You must use DQL/QueryBuilder.
- **No eager fetching of many-to-one AND collection in the same query** without cartesian issues.
- Doesn't help a single-entity `find()` at all.

### 2. Query Builder (programmatic version)

```php
$qb = $em->createQueryBuilder()
    ->select('o', 'li')
    ->from(Order::class, 'o')
    ->leftJoin('o.lineItems', 'li')
    ->where('o.id IN (:ids)')
    ->setParameter('ids', $ids)
    ->getQuery()
    ->getResult();
```

**Pros:** dynamic conditions, same single-query benefit.
**Cons:** same as DQL; you build the entity graph yourself.

### 3. `fetch = "EXTRA"` association mapping

```php
#[ORM\ManyToOne(...)]
#[ORM\ManyToMany(mappedBy: 'categories', fetch: 'EXTRA')]
private Collection $categories;
```

**Pros:** no query change needed, associations eager where possible.
**Cons:**
- **Still doesn't eager-load collections in a loop with pagination** — EXTRA triggers additional lazy loads but doesn't eliminate them in loops.
- **Cartesian products** with multiple associations.
- **Hard to reason about** — invisible queries.

### 4. DTO / partial object projection — when you only read

```php
$data = $em->createQuery(
    'SELECT new App\DTO\OrderRow(o.id, o.status, li.amount) ' .
    'FROM Order o JOIN o.lineItems li'
)->getResult();
```

**Pros:** minimal data transfer, no ORM overhead, can select only needed fields.
**Cons:**
- **Partial objects lose identity** — you get a projection, not the entity. Can't easily mutate and persist.
- **Two-phase:** if you later need the full entity, you re-fetch.
- DTO constructor must match the SELECT order.

### 5. GROUP BY dedup for pagination — the gotcha fix

The big gotcha: **`LEFT JOIN FETCH` + pagination → wrong COUNT.** The COUNT query counts pre-dedup rows, so your pagination is broken.

```php
// For COUNT: deduplicate
$qb->select('COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)')...

// For results with GROUP BY:
$qb->select('o')
   ->leftJoin('o.lineItems', 'li')
   ->groupBy('o.id')
   ->setFirstResult(...)
   ->setMaxResult(...);
```

**Pros:** correct pagination + eager collection in one query.
**Cons:**
- **GROUP BY hides the joined fields** — you must reference only the SELECTed entity's columns, or `GROUP BY o.id, li.amount` etc. (ugly, and breaks on non-selectable columns).
- Still a Cartesian product at DB level before dedup.

### 6. Second-level / query result cache

```php
$qb->useResultCache(true, 3600);
// or entity-level SecondLevelCache
```

**Pros:** zero queries on cache hits, great for rarely-changing data.
**Cons:** cache invalidation complexity, eventual staleness, must be configured carefully.

## Trade-off table

| Method | Query count | When to use | Main cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| `LEFT JOIN FETCH` | 1 | Read-heavy list of one type | Cartesian product, no `find()` |
| Query Builder | 1 | Dynamic conditions | Same as above |
| `fetch: EXTRA` | variable | Simple cases, minimal code | Invisible, still loads in loops |
| DTO projection | 1 | Read-only API/serialization | No entity identity, two-phase |
| GROUP BY | 1 + dedup | Pagination + eager | Ugly SELECT, partial results |
| Caches | 0 on hit | Stable, repeated queries | Invalidation, staleness |

## The key insight: fetch vs join cost

The trade-off isn't just "one query" — there are two costs:

- **Fetch strategy** (separate queries, e.g. a `SELECT` for all line_items): good for many associations, no cartesian products, but still N queries in loops unless batched.
- **Join strategy** (`JOIN FETCH`): single query but cartesian product growth.

**Rule of thumb:** if you're fetching **many associations per row**, fetch (separate query with `IN (...)`) beats join. If it's **one or two**, join. This is where `GROUP BY` and DTOs shine for pagination.

## My practical decision framework

1. **Confirm N+1 first** — don't optimize a mystery.
2. **If it's a loop over one entity type** → `LEFT JOIN FETCH` or Query Builder (single query).
3. **If pagination is involved** → the `JOIN FETCH` won't give correct counts; use `GROUP BY o.id` or fetch IDs, then fetch.
4. **If multiple associations or the projection is read-only** → DTO or separate-fetch pattern.
5. **If data is stable and repeatedly queried** → cache.
6. **Never cache the `em->find()` for eager collections** — find() returns lazy by default; you need DQL/QueryBuilder.

## Pitfalls to remember

1. **`em->find()` can't eager-load collections** — you need DQL/QueryBuilder for that.
2. **Cartesian products** with multiple `JOIN FETCH` — the hidden cost.
3. **Pagination COUNT wrong** after `LEFT JOIN FETCH` — deduplicate with `DISTINCT`.
4. **Fetching more associations than needed** — profile each, prune.
5. **Forgetting child collections** — eager `Order.lineItems` but lazy `LineItem.documents` is still N+1 one level down.
6. **Optimizing before measuring** — the lazy query might not be the bottleneck at all.

The short version: **`LEFT JOIN FETCH`/Query Builder for the common loop, GROUP BY for pagination, DTO for read-only projections, cache for stable data** — and always confirm the N+1 exists before optimizing.

Хотите — разберу конкретный кейс: например, список заказов со страницей с пагинацией и ленивыми LineItems + Document'ами, и покажу, как одним DQL'ом решить и N+1, и сломанную пагинацию, и cartesian-продукт?

## Q11 · PostgreSQL

**Интервьюер:** Вся бизнес-валидация делается в коде приложения. Зачем тогда ограничения целостности на уровне базы данных в PostgreSQL? Какие средства для этого используешь и в каких случаях?

<details><summary>Ход мысли модели</summary>

The user is asking a genuine architecture question: if business validation is done in application code, why do we still need database integrity constraints in PostgreSQL? What tools and when?

This is a thoughtful question that touches on the layered validation debate. Let me think carefully about the real answer.

First, the premise has a subtle flaw I should gently correct: application code validation is NOT a substitute for DB constraints because they serve fundamentally different purposes and have different threat models. Let me think about why constraints still matter:

1. **Defense in depth / last line of defense** — App validation can be bypassed. API endpoints, legacy code paths, direct DB access, migrations, concurrent transactions, third-party integrations, race conditions. The DB is the one thing that's always checked.

2. **Concurrent accesses / race conditions** — App-level checks (`SELECT ... WHERE ...`) are subject to TOCTOU (time-of-check-to-time-of-use) bugs. Two transactions can both see "the resource doesn't exist" and both insert. Only DB constraints (unique, FK, check) catch these atomically.

3. **Performance** — A CHECK constraint is cheaper than an application query. A unique index is enforced at storage level.

4. **Data integrity guarantees at rest** — constraints protect the data even if the app is wrong, buggy, or has a bug that only manifests later.

5. **Contract enforcement** — the schema is the real contract with every consumer of the DB, including batch jobs, analytics, external tools.

Now I should be honest about the tension too — there's a real debate here. Some argue that constraints duplicate validation and should be handled only by the domain. The key insight is that **these are two different kinds of validation**:
- Business/domain validation (invariants that require business logic, cross-entity rules, behavior) — belongs in app/domain.
- Integrity constraints (atomic, enforced at storage level) — belongs in DB.

Some rules can't be expressed in app code atomically (unique across partitions, FK relationships, not-null).

Let me also be honest about when NOT to put things in DB:
- Complex business rules that require joins across many tables or application state — hard to express as constraints, better in app.
- Rules that need to be versioned/tested with the app.
- Performance of constraint checking on huge tables.
- When constraints can't express the rule (e.g., temporal logic, cross-time).

Let me enumerate PostgreSQL tools:

1. **NOT NULL** — basic, can't be null.

2. **UNIQUE / PRIMARY KEY** — identity, uniqueness. Partial indexes for scoped uniqueness (e.g., unique email per tenant).

3. **FOREIGN KEY** — referential integrity. Enforces that child references exist. Also prevents orphan children (with ON DELETE RESTRICT/CASCADE). Cascade options: NO ACTION, RESTRICT, CASCADE, SET NULL.

4. **CHECK constraints** — boolean expressions on columns or row level. Examples: amount > 0, status IN (...), email format via regex, range checks.

5. **CHECK with CASE** — cross-column row-level: `CHECK (CASE WHEN type='credit' THEN amount > 0 ELSE amount < 0 END)`.

6. **Generated (computed) columns** — `GENERATED ALWAYS AS (expr) STORED/STORED`.

7. **Partial indexes** — `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ... WHERE active = true` for scoped uniqueness.

8. **Unique partial index** for business invariants like "one active subscription per user".

9. **Exclusion constraints** — `EXCLUDE USING GIST` for things like "overlapping reservations not allowed": `EXCLUDE (room_id WITH =,期间 WITH &&)`.

10. **Triggers** — complex cross-row logic, auditing, computed columns (before inserts), complex validations that CHECK can't express. Also can enforce cross-table invariants.

11. **PostgreSQL features**: domain types (CREATE DOMAIN), generated columns, etc.

12. **Foreign Data Wrappers** — less relevant.

Let me think about specific patterns:

- **Enumerations**: `status order_type CHECK (status IN ('pending','paid'))` vs using an enum type vs a separate lookup table. Postgres ENUM type vs CHECK with IN list vs reference table.

- **JSONB validation**: `CHECK (data ? 'required_key')`, `CHECK ((data->>'amount')::numeric > 0)`, JSONPath constraints in newer versions.

- **Postgres enum types** (`CREATE TYPE`).

- **Partitioning + constraints** — partition-level CHECK.

Let me think about a good worked example. Order/subscription is a classic:
- amount > 0 (CHECK)
- status IN enum (CHECK or enum type)
- email format
- unique active subscription per user (partial unique index)
- FK to customer, FK to order
- FK on delete behavior

Let me think about the trade-offs and when to use what:

**When constraints are the right answer:**
- Atomicity (unique, FK, check) — must be enforced at DB for race safety.
- Performance-critical cheap checks (not null, simple ranges).
- Data contracts for all consumers.
- Preventing corruption that app bugs could introduce.

**When NOT to use constraints:**
- Complex business rules needing application state/other tables' current state that changes.
- Rules that are frequently changing (schema migration overhead).
- Rules best tested in app with unit/integration tests.
- When the "rule" is really a behavior (e.g., "you can't cancel a refund after it's processed" — involves sequence of state changes that CHECK can't easily validate as it's a cross-tuple/stateful rule, though triggers could).

Actually triggers can express cross-tuple logic, but they're hard to maintain and test. Trade-off: triggers give you DB-enforced complex logic but at cost of debuggability, versioning (migration tooling), and testing.

1. **Input validation** — is the request well-formed? (app, UX)
2. **Domain invariants** — business rules. (app/domain, often stateful)
3. **Integrity** — atomic storage guarantees. (DB)

These overlap but aren't the same. A good answer distinguishes them.

One important nuance: app validation catches errors EARLIER with BETTER error messages and USER-CONTEXT, while DB validation is the GUARANTEE. They complement. And there's a subtle point: DB constraints fire with confusing generic errors ("duplicate key", "constraint failed") which you must translate back into domain exceptions. That's the cost of DB constraints — the app must catch them and rethrow domain exceptions.
